10.8    Completion of the proof
Proof of Theorem 1.1. Let K ⊂ R4 contain a unit line segment in every direction. Suppose that
dimH K < 4, and choose σ > 0 with dimH K < 4 − σ. By Theorem 2.17, the Hausdorff covers of
this arbitrary set produce an exact problem of the second model in C(d0 ), for some d0 < 1 and
fixed positive angular parameters, with H > 0. That reduction uses outer slope measure and finite
selections of witnesses, so it applies to nonmeasurable K and nonmeasurable witnessing line families.
    Optimize cap dimension and horizon as in Proposition 4.3. The resulting critical dimension is
less than one and the maximizing time rate satisfies k > 0. The auxiliary first model has already
been excluded by Corollary 6.9; this supplies the scalar results used in the subsequent second-model
branches.
    For the second model, consider the global optimized narrowness ℓ. If ℓ = 0, Theorem 6.1
gives a forbidden improvement. For 0 < ℓ < ∞, the projection and comparison arguments in
Propositions 8.4 and 8.5 either give such an improvement immediately or reduce to the horizontal
case with narrowness rate equal to k. At 2k = 1, Proposition 9.2 excludes that case. Above the
critical rate, Proposition 9.6 supplies the wide intervals required by Proposition 8.4. Below the
critical rate, Proposition 9.5 first reaches the limiting parameter scale, and Proposition 9.6 then
supplies those intervals. In each instance the original-unit construction of Proposition 4.11 yields the
actual atlas forbidden by the critical-path optimization. Finally, the global case ℓ = ∞ is excluded
by Proposition 10.1.
    Every possible optimized narrowness has therefore been excluded, contradicting the positive-
horizon problem supplied by Theorem 2.17. It follows that dimH K ≥ 4. The ambient upper bound
is dimH K ≤ 4, so dimH K = 4. The argument imposes no compactness, measurability or stickiness
condition on K or on its witnessing line family.


11     Geometric consequences
Theorem 1.1 has consequences for other notions of dimension, for unions of segments with prescribed
directions, and for several curved incidence problems. The direction-set and curved conclusions use
the transfer theorems of Keleti and Máthé, Gao, Liu, and Xi, and Nadjimzadah.

11.1    Packing and Minkowski dimensions
Every Kakeya set K ⊂ R4 also has packing dimension four, since Hausdorff dimension is at most
packing dimension, which is at most the ambient dimension. If the set is bounded, both its lower and


                                                 168
upper Minkowski dimensions are four: Hausdorff dimension is at most lower Minkowski dimension,
and lower Minkowski dimension is at most upper Minkowski dimension, which is at most four.
Here the lower and upper Minkowski dimensions are respectively the lower and upper limits of
log Nδ (K)/ log(1/δ), where Nδ (K) is the least number of balls of radius δ covering K, as δ ↓ 0.
Thus the theorem also controls the common-scale covering growth of each bounded Kakeya set.

11.2    Projection to four dimensions
Theorem 1.1 also implies that every Kakeya set E ⊂ Rd , d ≥ 4, has dimH E ≥ 4; in particular,
this holds in dimension five. Fix a four-dimensional linear subspace V , and let PV be orthogonal
projection onto V . For each unit v ∈ V , a witnessing segment a + [0, 1]v ⊂ E projects to the unit
segment PV a + [0, 1]v. Hence PV E is a Kakeya set in V ≃ R4 , so 4 = dimH (PV E) ≤ dimH E. The
final inequality follows because projection does not increase covering diameters, with no compactness
or measurability assumption. The resulting lower bound is four; the full conjecture in Rd asks for
dimension d.

11.3    Arbitrary directions and extension of segments
Keleti and Máthé transfer the full-direction assertion to arbitrary direction sets. We identify
unoriented directions in R4 with the real projective space RP3 .

Corollary 11.1 (General direction-set bound). Let D ⊂ RP3 be nonempty and let E ⊂ R4 . If E
contains a nondegenerate line segment in every direction in D, then

                                       dimH E ≥ 1 + dimH D.

Proof. By Keleti and Máthé [20, Theorem 1.4], there is a compact Besicovitch set B ⊂ R4 such that
dimH E ≥ dimH B − (3 − dimH D). Their Besicovitch convention requires a unit segment in every
direction, so Theorem 1.1 gives dimH B = 4. Substitution proves the bound.

   In particular, a direction set of Hausdorff dimension three already forces full spatial dimension.
We will use this below when the directions form a nonempty open patch. Neither E nor D is
required to be measurable, and the positive segment lengths may vary.
   The same conclusion gives a line-segment extension principle by the implication proved in Keleti
and Máthé [20, Section 1.2]. For any nonempty family S of nondegenerate segments in R4 , let L be
the family of their supporting full lines. Then
                                          [              [     
                                   dimH         S = dimH         L .
                                          S∈S              L∈L

Thus extending every segment in a fixed family to its whole line preserves the Hausdorff dimension
of the union. This application uses the authors’ same-dimensional implication from the general
direction-set bound.

11.4    Nikodym sets on constant-curvature manifolds
The Nikodym formulation selects geodesics through a positive-volume family of base points, rather
than selecting a segment for every direction. Theorem 1.1 controls this different incidence requirement
through the established Kakeya-to-Nikodym implication of Gao, Liu, and Xi. We use their normalized
local geodesic formulation [11, Definition 1.3 and Theorem 2.1]: the metric is normalized so that


                                                     169
the injectivity radius is at least 10, and the geodesic segments below have unit length. For a Borel
set Ω ⊂ M and 0 < λ < 1, put
                        (                                                               )
                                  there is a unit geodesic segment γx through x
                Ω⋆λ =       x∈M :                                                           ,
                                  such that |γx ∩ Ω|g ≥ λ|γx |g

where the bars denote geodesic length. The set Ω is a Nikodym set if Ω⋆λ has positive Riemannian
volume for every λ sufficiently close to 1.

Corollary 11.2 (Four-dimensional Nikodym dimension). Let (M 4 , g) be a Riemannian manifold of
constant sectional curvature in the preceding normalized setting. Every Nikodym set Ω ⊂ M has
dimH Ω = 4.

Proof. Theorem 1.1 applies in particular to the compact Euclidean Kakeya sets in the convention
of Gao et al. [11, Definition 1.1]. It therefore supplies their Kakeya Hausdorff lower bound with
d = α = 4. Gao et al. [11, Theorem 1.5] transfers this bound to Nikodym sets on constant-sectional-
curvature d-manifolds, giving dimH Ω ≥ 4. The reverse inequality holds for every subset of a
four-dimensional Riemannian manifold.

   This resolves Conjecture 1.4 of Gao et al. [11] in dimension four and includes Euclidean R4 . The
geodesic straightening and the Kakeya-to-Nikodym transfer are results of those authors; no new
transfer argument or variable-curvature assertion is made here.

11.5    Curved Kakeya sets for a fixed translation-invariant phase
A separate consequence concerns the structured curved-Kakeya class of Gao, Liu, and Xi. Unlike
the preceding Nikodym corollary, it uses a direction-indexed family from one phase in a Euclidean
chart. We use their phase and core conventions [11, Definitions 1.7–1.11], with the active support
made explicit. One common local change of coordinates straightens a direction patch of this family.
This lets the Euclidean dimension theorem control the original curved set through local bi-Lipschitz
invariance.
    Write X = (x, t) ∈ R3 × R, and fix a sufficiently small chart

                                       U = Bρ3 × (−ρ, ρ),       Y = Bρ3 ,

where Bρ3 is the open Euclidean ball about the origin. Fix one smooth phase on a neighborhood of
U ×Y,
                            ϕ(x, t; y) = x · y + ψ(t; y),  ψ(0; y) = 0.

hypotheses, let E ⊂ R4 . Suppose that for every y ∈ Y there exists ωy ∈ Bρ3 such that

                                               Γϕy ((ωy , 0)) ⊂ E.

Then dimH E = 4.

Proof. For this phase, G0 = (−∂t ∇y ψ, 1) in the standard orientation. The mixed rank is automatic,
and the remaining conditions become

                    det ∇2y ∂t ψ(t; y) ̸= 0,       ∇2y ∂t2 ψ(t; y) = c(t, y)∇2y ∂t ψ(t; y).

The nonsingular matrix determines this scalar smoothly and independently of x. Gao et al. [11,
Corollary 4.2 and Theorem 1.12] show that c depends only on t on the connected local product and
straighten the phase. More precisely, their proof gives, after shrinking to an open time interval I
containing 0 and a nonempty direction ball Y0 ⊂ Y ,

                     κ(u, s) = (u + B(α(s)), α(s)),            α(0) = 0,      α′ (s) ̸= 0,

                  ϕ(κ(u, s); y) = u · y + sh(y) + q(y) + f (s),           det ∇2y h(y) ̸= 0,
whenever s ∈ I, y ∈ Y0 , and κ(u, s) ∈ U . This is one smooth coordinate change for the fixed phase.
   Since ψ(0; y) = 0, the core through (ωy , 0) is

                              Γϕy ((ωy , 0)) = {(ωy − ∇y ψ(t; y), t) ∈ U }.

Put V = U ∩ (R3 × α(I)) and F = κ−1 (E ∩ V ). Every anchor (ωy , 0) lies in the open set V , so
continuity supplies a nontrivial closed time interval on which its core remains in V . In the new
coordinates the gradient equality defining that core is exactly

                                      u = (ωy − B(0)) − s∇h(y).

Indeed, the ∇q(y) terms cancel from the two gradient values, and f (s) has zero y-gradient. Thus F
contains a positive-length straight segment in the direction
                                     (−∇h(y), 1)
                             ν(y) = p                        for every y ∈ Y0 .
                                      1 + |∇h(y)|2
The nonsingular Hessian of h makes ν a local diffeomorphism. Its image therefore contains a
nonempty open patch in the upper hemisphere of S 3 . The corresponding unoriented directions form
an open set D ⊂ RP3 , so dimH D = 3. Corollary 11.1 gives dimH F = 4; it allows the positive
segment lengths to depend on y.

                                                      171
   A smooth local diffeomorphism preserves Hausdorff dimension of arbitrary subsets, using a
countable cover by coordinate neighborhoods on which it and its inverse are Lipschitz. Consequently

                            4 = dimH F = dimH (E ∩ V ) ≤ dimH E ≤ 4.

No compactness or measurability of E or of y 7→ ωy , and no uniform lower bound on the initial
segment lengths, was used.

   The straightening and general-dimensional set transfer are due to Gao et al. [11, Theorem 1.12
and Section 4.2]. The corollary applies their transfer to full gradient-defined cores in one fixed active
chart.
   The translation-invariance assumption specifies the class being straightened here. Bourgain’s
condition alone need not allow one coordinate change to straighten the curves, as the examples of
Nadjimzadah [22, Example 1.24 and Proposition 1.25] show.

11.6    A three-dimensional quadratic curved consequence
A further application uses Nadjimzadah’s lifting theorem to pass from the four-dimensional linear
result to curved sets in dimension three. For a real 2 × 2 matrix B, let I2 denote the identity matrix.
Use the fixed compact anchor ball WB ⊂ R2 , chosen sufficiently large, and the fixed time interval
IB = [−τB , τB ], with τB > 0 sufficiently small, specified after equation (1.16) of Nadjimzadah [23].

Corollary 11.4 (Rank-one quadratic curved Kakeya sets). Let B be a real 2 × 2 matrix with
rank B = rank(B 2 ) = 1, and use the fixed domains just specified. Suppose that a compact set
K ⊂ R3 contains, for every y ∈ [−1, 1]2 , the full curve

                                   {(wy + (tI2 + t2 B)y, t) : t ∈ IB }

for some wy ∈ WB . Then dimH K = 3.

Proof. Corollary 11.1 supplies the linear Kakeya hypothesis in the convention of Nadjimzadah [23,
Section 1.2, equations (1.8)–(1.13)]. Indeed, a compact linear-chart set F ⊂ R4 in that convention
contains a segment
                                        {(bη + tη, t) : t ∈ J}
for every slope η in a fixed three-dimensional parameter ball, with anchors in a fixed compact ball
and one fixed nondegenerate compact interval J. Slopes in the interior give a nonempty open set
of directions [η : 1] ∈ RP3 , and every segment has positive length. The corollary therefore gives
dimH F = 4.
    The geometry of Nadjimzadah’s quadratic lift explains the two rank hypotheses. Write B = U V ,
where U : R → R2 is injective and V : R2 → R is surjective, and define

                      π : R2 × R × R −→ R2 × R,           π(x, z, t) = (x + tU z, t).

The lifted segments in Nadjimzadah [23, equations (4.1)–(4.9)] are

                          Ly,w,r (t) = (w, r) + t(y − U r, V y), t ,
                                                                  
                                                                         t ∈ IB ,

with r in a fixed compact interval with interior. They map to the prescribed curves over the entire
time interval:
                    π(Ly,w,r (t)) = (w + ty + t2 U V y, t) = (w + (tI2 + t2 B)y, t).


                                                   172
Since B 2 = U (V U )V has rank one, V U ̸= 0. The slope map Θ(y, r) = (y − U r, V y) consequently
satisfies                                             !
                                              I2 −U
                              det DΘ = det              = V U ̸= 0.
                                              V    0
Thus varying y and r in their interiors produces an open slope patch in dimension four. Definition 2.1
of Nadjimzadah [23] retains every t ∈ IB in these lifted segments, each of which has length at least
2τB .
   With the four-dimensional linear hypothesis established, Nadjimzadah [23, Theorem 1.3] applies
with 3 + rank(B 2 ) = 4. It gives infimal Hausdorff dimension 3 − rank B + rank(B 2 ) = 3 for the
compact curved sets in the statement. Hence dimH K ≥ 3, and the ambient bound gives equality.

     This is a lower-dimensional application of the headline result: the prescribed quadratic curves
lift to linear Kakeya sets in one additional dimension. Compactness and containment of the full
curves over the fixed interval are part of this application. No regularity of the anchor choice y 7→ wy
is required.
